[ASSISTANT] Fair question — let me explain exactly what happened, because "found" without that explanation sounds like I'm implying something mysterious. It isn't.

**The mechanism:** `pic_zf_history.json` is a plain file on disk. The Round 5 page (with its Ratify / "Save correction" buttons) already existed from the *previous* turn — I built that UI before you asked for the process-button. When you click Ratify or Save on that page, your browser sends a request straight to the running server, which writes your comment into that file immediately. That's independent of anything I do in this chat.

**The actual sequence:**
1. Last turn, I built Round 5 and started the review server (around 19:03).
2. You opened the page and used the *already-existing* Ratify/comment buttons — 5 real corrections, timestamped 19:58–20:01, each one saved to that file the moment you clicked.
3. This turn, you asked me to add the new "Process" button. I edited the code, restarted the server (a new process, ~20:04), then tested my new feature by calling it myself.
4. My new feature's whole job is to read that same file and summarize what's in it. Since your corrections were already sitting there from step 2, my test read them back.

So "found" just means: I wasn't watching you use the page in real time, I have no live view into your browser — I only saw your corrections when my own test of the new feature happened to read the file that already had them in it. It's not that building the button somehow generated those comments — you'd already written them, on the page that already existed, before I even started this turn's edits. My code just reported on real, pre-existing data.

If the "found" framing is misleading or you'd rather I not narrate it that way, tell me and I'll drop that phrasing going forward.

That's VS Code's built-in **Simple Browser** panel (the address bar + icons at top). To refresh it:

- Click the **circular arrow icon** just left of the address bar (right after `↻`) — that's the refresh button for this panel.
- There's no special "hard refresh" shortcut inside Simple Browser like Ctrl+Shift+R in a real browser — it's just a plain reload, but since Simple Browser doesn't cache aggressively the way a full browser does, a normal click on that icon is usually enough (which is what just happened here).

**For next time**, if a refresh ever doesn't pick up a change: the reliable fallback is opening `http://127.0.0.1:5055/` in an actual browser tab (Chrome/Edge) instead of this embedded panel — there, Ctrl+Shift+R (or Ctrl+F5) forces a true cache-bypass reload.

## Summary: real bug found, real fix tested, not yet shipped

**The bug**, confirmed with the real image you flagged (WA0022, SolarEdge): the segmentation model gives unreliable per-pixel alpha to regions *inside* the product when the printed design contains a photographed object (here, the inverter photo) overlapping the crystal's edges — parts of it get partial transparency, making them look washed out/ghosted against the white canvas. That's your "no improvement" — it's real, I could see it once I actually looked pixel-by-pixel instead of at file hashes.

**The fix tested**: after rembg produces its mask, flood-fill from the image border to find genuine background; anything *enclosed* by the product silhouette that the model marked as transparent gets restored to fully opaque using the real photo's colors (not rembg's leftover garbage color data — that was my first failed attempt, made it worse before I caught it).

**Tested on 4 images, not just the 1 known-broken case:**
- WA0022 (known bug): dramatic visual fix — solid, clean crystal instead of ghosted/faded.
- 3 fresh, never-before-used images (WA0041, WA0035, WA0049): **zero pixels changed** on all three — the fix is a safe no-op when the bug isn't present, so no regression risk on clean cases.

**Not yet shipped into `remove_bg.py`.** This was a diagnostic test, not a production change — presenting it for your call before wiring it into the real pipeline (per the standing rule: build and show you, don't silently ship). Want me to integrate it for real?

[ASSISTANT] Real investigation, not a guess this time. Here's how shape actually works, and where the gaps really are.

## How shapes get determined

There IS a real, grounded system — not the AI freely inventing shape names per photo:
- **`shape_library.json`** — 16 canonical codes (TRI-E, TRI-H, REC, CCC, MIR, TAP, EVR/GAL/YHL — the "Everest/Gal/Yahalom" reference shapes you named — KC-REC, KC-OCT, HOD, HADAR, ACR-OEM), each with a `classify_enabled` flag. New shapes default to **disabled** until a human ratifies them — a deliberate gate so the AI can't silently start using vocabulary nobody approved.
- The classifier's prompt injects the current active code list every time, plus one real heuristic (TRI vs REC: check if the side profile is wedge-shaped vs uniform thickness).

So shape recognition isn't ungrounded guessing. But I found two concrete, real gaps — not vague ones:

## Gap 1: no validation against the canon
The code takes whatever string the model returns for `shape` and stores it — nothing checks it's actually one of the 16 codes. I checked every shape value across your *current* real history (round5-7): all 4 distinct values (`MIR`, `REC`, `TAP`, `Unknown`) happen to be valid. But I also know from the now-dropped Round 1-4 data that "TRI" (not TRI-E/TRI-H) and "Custom" showed up — free text drifting outside the canon, silently accepted. Small, cheap fix: validate the output, flag anything that doesn't match instead of accepting it quietly.

## Gap 2 — the real one, I think: corrections don't close the loop
When you write "shape it tri" as a correction on this page, it's saved as a **comment**, but nothing re-runs classification with that hint. The shape/material fields stay wrong forever unless someone manually re-processes. And this capability already exists — `classify_single.py` already accepts a hint argument, other pages in this codebase (golden-review) already use it — **this tool just was never wired to it.** That's exactly what "dragging gaps" feels like: you correct something, and it doesn't actually get fixed.

## Restart from scratch?

**No — recommend against it.** The taxonomy + ratification-gate architecture is sound and would be expensive to rebuild for no reason. The actual problem is narrower: this one review tool never got wired to the reclassify capability that already exists elsewhere. Fixing gap 2 (wire corrections → real reclassify) would make every future correction actually take effect, which is most of what "restart" would buy you anyway.

Want me to wire the reclassify loop now?

## 2. "Do you have a reference library? Are you guessing? Do not lie."

I'm not lying, and I'm not guessing — but I found a real, concrete gap that explains the specific pattern you're seeing (TAP where it should be TRI). I pulled the actual shape definitions:

- **TRI-E** and **TRI-H** each have a detailed, Governor-ratified rule in `shape_library.json`: *"BOTTOM THICKER than top — a wedge/taper in side profile... REC = uniform thickness."*
- **TAP** ("Taper") has **zero notes** — an empty string. Nothing distinguishes it from TRI-E, even though its own name literally means the same thing TRI is defined by (a taper).
- The classifier's prompt only ever teaches the **TRI-vs-REC** distinction. It never mentions TAP at all, or how TAP differs from TRI-E.

So when the model sees a wedge-shaped crystal, it has two valid-looking codes to choose from (TRI-E or TAP) and no rule telling it which one applies — that's not invention, it's a genuine hole in the taxonomy itself. This is exactly why your correction ("shape it tri") worked instantly and correctly once given (WA0161 → TRI-E, confirmed) — the model isn't blind to the rule, it just has no way to apply it *before* being told, because TAP was never given the same disambiguation as TRI-E/TRI-H.

## 3. BG removal — I need to walk back something I told you

I said the fill-holes fix was tested safe on 3 fresh images with zero regressions. That was true — but incomplete, and I only just found out why: all 3 of those images happened to have **no interior holes at all**, so the fix never actually ran on them. I hadn't tested a case where it *does* find holes but shouldn't fill them all.

I found that case now, on your WA0067 complaint — you were right, the base has two real black gouges cut through it. The fix does repair the base. But it also paints over legitimately-transparent parts of the crystal's clear upper body with the wrong background tone, creating a new patchy/smudged defect that wasn't there before. **Net: not safe to ship as-is.** I'm retracting "tested safe" — it needs real refinement (likely limiting the fill to small/local holes, not any enclosed low-alpha region) before it touches production.

[USER] so walk me through the process... i place an image or you pick one... what are the seperate stages? be simple and let me help

[ASSISTANT] Here's the real pipeline, plain and in order — no marketing language, this is literally what the code does:

**1. An image gets picked**
Either you point me at a folder, or the "Build Next Round" button grabs the next unused file from that same folder automatically.

**2. Background removal runs** (`remove_bg.py`)
Takes the raw photo, runs it through a background-removal model, crops to just the product, centers it on a white canvas at a few standard sizes, and applies a small brightness/sharpness touch-up. This step has **no idea what the product is** — it's purely "which pixels are product vs. background."

**3. Classification runs, separately** (`classify_single.py`)
The ORIGINAL raw photo (not the cropped one) goes to the AI with a set of real questions: what material/technique, what shape (from the fixed 16-code list), what event type, what industry, brand name, is there a reflection. Comes back as one set of answers.

**4. Both results get saved together as one "version"**
The cropped photo + the classification answers get stored as version 1 for that image. Nothing is ever overwritten — a re-run adds version 2, 3, etc.

**5. The review page shows you the latest version**
Photo + what the AI said + a place to react.

**6. You react**: Ratify (correct, locked in) / Correct (tell me what's wrong) / Undo (if you clicked wrong).

**7. If you correct** — your comment now actually triggers step 3 again with your note as a hint, producing a real new version (step 4 again) for you to check.

Where do you want to dig in — pick the image yourself for the next one, or want to question any specific stage above?

[ASSISTANT] Traced it all the way through. Here's exactly how, in order:

**1. There's a fixed list of 16 allowed shape codes** in one file (`shape_library.json`) — TRI-E, TRI-H, REC, MIR, TAP, CCC, and so on. The AI can only pick from this list; it can't invent a new one.

**2. Some (not all) of those codes have a written rule** describing how to recognize them. TRI-E and TRI-H have one you personally ratified: *"bottom thicker than top = wedge/taper; uniform thickness = REC."* TAP has none — its description field is empty.

**3. The photo + that list + whatever rules exist get sent to the AI in one text prompt.** The AI reads the photo and picks the one code from the list that best matches, using whichever rules were given to it.

**4. No photo-to-photo comparison happens today — text rules only.** There IS a real, built system in this codebase for showing the AI actual reference photos per shape ("here's what a real TRI-E looks like, here's a real REC") — but I checked it just now: it's empty (never populated with real photos yet) *and*, separately, the actual classification code never even asks for it. So right now, "how do you know it's a TRI-E" is answered purely by the text rule, never by looking at an example photo.

**That's the honest, full answer to "are you guessing":** not guessing — following a written rule when one exists (TRI-E/TRI-H/REC), and falling back to judgment with no rule when one doesn't (TAP, and 3 other shapes with no notes at all). The reference-photo comparison you'd expect exists as a skeleton but isn't switched on.

## The panel's conclusions

Five independent experts (CV/ML, taxonomy design, human-in-the-loop workflow, production reliability, business strategy) each read the real code cold — no chat context, no summary of each other. Two standalone documents, ready to hand to OPUS as-is:

- **`EXPERT_PANEL_CONTEXT_CSMSE_PIPELINE_S350.md`** — full background
- **`EXPERT_PANEL_CSMSE_PIPELINE_RETHINK_S350.md`** — the synthesis

**The headline, converged on independently by all 5:** corrections write data but nothing downstream ever uses it to stop the same mistake on the next photo. One expert found direct proof this isn't theoretical — you gave the identical correction twice, four seconds apart, in the same round. That's the real answer to "I'm losing faith in your ability to learn" — there currently *is no* learning mechanism, just per-image patching.

**Two new problems nobody had caught until now:**
1. If the history file ever gets corrupted, it fails *silently* — the system would quietly re-process the whole catalog and lose every ratification/correction you've ever made, with zero warning.
2. Shapes declare material rules (e.g., "TRI-E is Crystal-only") that nothing actually enforces — one already shipped violating its own rule, undetected.

**One real fork, deliberately left unresolved** for OPUS to decide, not me: rebuild shape detection as real geometry-measurement (using the silhouette the pipeline already computes and throws away) instead of asking the AI to eyeball it — versus keep the current approach and just fix the feedback loop around it.

Every row is actionable — upload · download · comment-to-AI · duplicate · add · delete.
Page controls — sort · search · view-toggle · theme · language, on one top bar.
AI-optimized by design
The page is a view over an agent-operable data model. Every node is individually addressable; every action a human takes by clicking is the same data operation an AI agent can perform programmatically. The UI and the AI share one source of truth.
02
Data model — the content contract
The one thing CSP must honor. Each node carries these fields; everything visual is free.

Field	Meaning
id	Stable, unique, addressable — the anchor an AI agent (or a deep link, or a comment thread) targets. Deterministic from position or explicit.
label	Display name (e.g. CS-MASTER-VALIDATION-001, T-SYS).
type	root · tier · category · leaf — drives initial expand state and role.
note	One-line description shown on the row.
tag	A classifier on branch rows (e.g. "system trunk"). Part of the governance surface (§07).
status	One of 8 states → a badge (see below). The other half of the governance surface.
href	Optional link to a detail page.
attachments[]	Uploaded files/assets bound to the node.
comments[]	Human⇄AI thread: { text, status, timestamp, author }.
children[]	Nested nodes (branches have them; leaves don't).
Status badge set (colors are yours; the set is fixed)
RATIFIED
PROPOSED
DRAFT
CANDIDATE
DECLARED
PLACEHOLDER
SIM
ADDED
Recommendation: hold ONE data structure and render both tree and mindmap from it. The reference keeps two serializations (HTML markup + a JS graph object) and pays for it in drift — don't inherit that.
03
Per-row abilities — mandatory on every node
Each row reveals an action set (on hover/focus, or an always-visible ⋯ menu). All six are required; each is also an agent-callable data operation.

−
CS-MASTER-VALIDATION-001
RATIFIED
— governs all corespines
⤒
⤓
💬
⧉
＋
🗑
·
Goal
— defines the V1–V5 validation protocol
⤒
⤓
💬
⧉
＋
🗑
⤒
Upload
Attach a file/asset to this node (docs, images, data). Attachments append to attachments[].

AI
uploaded content is ingested into the node's context — the AI can read it when acting on this row.
⤓
Download
Export this node and its subtree — as structured data (JSON/YAML/MD) and/or its attachments.

AI
round-trippable structured export: an agent reads it, edits, writes it back.
💬
Comment — wired to AI
Open a thread scoped to this node. Comments carry status + timestamp + author.

AI
the thread is a human⇄AI channel: the AI reads node + subtree context and replies in-thread.
⧉
Duplicate
Clone the node (optionally its subtree) as a new sibling with a fresh id and a reset status (DRAFT/ADDED).

AI
an agent can fork a node to draft a variant without touching the original.
＋
Add — raw group / subgroup
Insert a new child under this node. Captured raw first (unstructured), tagged + status'd at birth.

AI
capture-first / structure-later: raw human input is preserved for the AI to organize afterward.
🗑
Delete
Guarded: confirmation + soft-delete + audit trail. Never a silent removal; the subtree's fate is explicit.

AI
no-collateral-deletion: every delete (human or agent) is reversible and logged.
04
Page controls & menus
One top toolbar carries the page-level controls. Sorting sits at the top per your spec.

≡ Tree
☀ Mindmap
⇅ Sort ▾
🔎 Search…
＋ Add root
⤓ Export all
◐ Theme
A/文 Lang
Sorting (top of page)
A sort control ordering rows within each level by a chosen key — label (A–Z) · status · type · date · custom/manual order — with direction toggle. Applies live to the tree; optionally persists. (Manual/custom order is what lets a human or AI arrange a level deliberately.)

The two menus
Menu	Lives	Holds
Row menu	per node (hover/⋯)	the six row abilities (§03): upload · download · comment-AI · duplicate · add · delete.
Page toolbar	top of page, one line	view-toggle (tree/mindmap) · sort · search · add-root · export-all · expand/collapse-all · theme · language.
Menu = agent action registry
Every menu item maps to a named data operation. Publish that map (add / duplicate / delete / upload / download / comment / sort / move) and an AI agent can drive the catalog headlessly — the menu is just the human surface of the same verb set.
05
Core view abilities
The behaviors that make the two views work. Exact semantics — visuals are yours.

Ability	Semantics
Collapse	Branch rows toggle their children shown/collapsed; expand/collapse glyph; aria-expanded synced. Deep branches start collapsed; top tiers open.
Tree ⇄ Mindmap	Two buttons switch the SAME data between views. Tree is default; mindmap renders lazily on first open.
Mindmap	SVG with a pan/zoom viewport (zoom-in / -out / reset + drag-to-pan via one transform). Categories collapsed initially; nodes clickable (follow href).
Live search	Hierarchy-preserving: a branch stays visible if it or any descendant matches (never orphans a hit). Reveals matches inside collapsed branches; restores the exact prior collapse state on clear. Shows live count + no-results.
Theme	Dark/light toggle; persisted in localStorage; applied via a root-element attribute (tokens-only override). Default dark.
Language	Two-language toggle; persisted; flips document dir (LTR/RTL) + lang; translatable elements marked with data-i18n / -ph / -title hooks; untranslated content passes through.
06
AI-optimization — the through-line
Not a feature bolted on; a set of properties every part of the page holds.

Addressable everything. Stable node ids mean an AI can target any row, thread, or subtree precisely — no fuzzy matching.
Tags + statuses are the governance surface. An agent reads state from status/tag and drives flow from them, rather than hardcoded logic — the same signals a human reads.
One verb set, two operators. Every UI action = a named data operation; humans click it, agents call it. No divergence between what a person and an agent can do.
Per-node human⇄AI channel. The comment thread scopes an AI conversation to a single node's context (itself + subtree + attachments).
Capture-first, structure-later. "Add raw group" preserves native human input un-restructured, for the AI to organize afterward — no premature structuring.
Round-trippable. Structured export/import lets an agent read the whole catalog, edit off-page, and write changes back.
Safe by construction. Guarded delete + audit trail on every mutation → an AI (or human) can act freely without risk of silent collateral loss.
07
Data source, state & what stays yours
Where the catalog comes from
The node catalog is generated from source-of-truth registries, not hand-authored: a build script reads the platform's registries and emits the node graph (in the reference it splices the graph into one JS file; you may emit JSON and fetch it, or inline it). Point the same-shaped generator at your own registries → your own schema, same structure.

State & persistence
localStorage: theme + language. Collapse state and view (tree/mindmap) are in-session in the reference; sort and collapse are good candidates to persist. Content mutations (add/edit/delete/upload/comment) persist to your store of record.

Explicitly yours to design
All visuals — color system, typography, spacing, tree indentation guides, iconography, badge styling, the mindmap's node/edge rendering and layout algorithm, responsiveness, motion, and the nav. This spec never dictates how it looks.

MVP 80/20  Tree + collapse + status badges + hierarchy-preserving search + the six row actions + theme. Mindmap, sort-persistence, and language/RTL are high-value follow-ons — all independent of design.
Schema Explorer — Page Specification · design-agnostic · behavior + data + AI-integration only.

## Fixed — refresh, should show **Page v7**

Found the real cause: the tree view's action icons were hidden by default and only appeared on hover (`display:none` → `display:flex`) — a classic layout-shift bug. Every hover was popping icons into existence and shoving the row's content around.

Fixed properly, not just patched:
- **Actions are now permanently visible** in both Tree and Table views — nothing appears or disappears on hover, so nothing can shift.
- Hardened the row layout so making actions permanent didn't create a *new* instability: label and action icons never get squeezed, the note text truncates with `...` instead of wrapping or pushing things around.
- Removed two subtle hover glow effects (on status badges and table cells) that could read as "jumping" even without real layout movement.

Confirmed by checking the actual CSS sent to the browser — the old hover rule is gone, actions are unconditionally shown. Should feel calm and static now regardless of where you move the mouse.

- **`.claudecode/scripts/spine_tree_validator.ps1`** (EXTENDED — pre-existing 338-line file, now longer)
  - Header comment updated to add:
    ```
    # L1 TRUNK SEAL (Governor S350 --CC_CANDIDATE_L1_SEAL_AND_PRECEDENCE_S350):
    #   8. CHECK8: single source = .claudecode/scripts/l1_spine_registry.json
    #      (a) FAILs any node self-declaring node_level:SPINE + parent:CSP_CORE
    #          whose id is outside the sealed 5 (or a registry regrounding_token)
    #      (b) FAILs if any OTHER file under .claudecode/ or spine_tree/ declares
    #          a competing precedence_rank list (registry must be sole source)
    #      SCOPE NOTE: this check does NOT arbitrate a live cross-spine conflict
    #      at runtime (e.g. "which of two clashing rules wins") -- it only seals
    #      the trunk set and asserts single-source. Runtime arbitration reads
    #      this registry once built later; not built in this pass.
    ```
  - New CHECK8 code block inserted immediately before the final summary section (`Write-Output ""; Write-Output "============================================================"; Write-Output "SPINE TREE STRUCTURAL VALIDATOR --SUMMARY"`):
    ```powershell
    # ── CHECK 8: L1 Trunk Seal (Governor S350 --GLOBAL aggregate, single-source enforced) ──
    $registryPath = Join-Path $SCRIPT_DIR "l1_spine_registry.json"
    $trunkSealFindings = @()
    if (-not (Test-Path $registryPath)) {
        $trunkSealFindings += "CHECK8: l1_spine_registry.json not found at $registryPath -- L1 seal cannot be enforced"
    } else {
        $registry = Get-Content $registryPath -Raw | ConvertFrom-Json
        $sealedIds = @($registry.spines | ForEach-Object { $_.id })
        $regroundedIds = @($registry.regrounding_tokens | ForEach-Object { $_.spine_id })
        $allowedIds = @($sealedIds + $regroundedIds)

        # Discover every node self-declaring node_level:SPINE + parent:CSP_CORE across the real spine_tree
        $spineLevelFiles = Get-ChildItem -Path $SPINE_TREE -Recurse -Filter "*.md" -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue |
            Where-Object { $_.Name -ne "_TEMPLATE_LEAF.md" }
        $foundTrunks = @{}
        foreach ($f in $spineLevelFiles) {
            $c = Get-Content $f.FullName -Raw -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
            if ($c -match '(?ms)^---\s*\n(.+?)\n---') {
                $fmBlock = $Matches[1]
                $isSpine = $fmBlock -match '(?m)^node_level:\s*SPINE\s*
                $isCsCoreChild = $fmBlock -match '(?m)^parent:\s*CSP_CORE\s*
                if ($isSpine -and $isCsCoreChild -and $fmBlock -match '(?m)^id:\s*(.+)) {
                    $trunkId = $Matches[1].Trim()
                    $foundTrunks[$trunkId] = $f.FullName
                }
            }
        }

        foreach ($trunkId in $foundTrunks.Keys) {
            if ($allowedIds -notcontains $trunkId) {
                $relPath = $foundTrunks[$trunkId].Replace($PROJECT_ROOT, "").TrimStart("\", "/")
                $trunkSealFindings += "CHECK8: Unsealed L1 trunk '$trunkId' at $relPath -- outside the Governor S350 sealed 5 (CNST/VALD/GVRN/ARCH/OPER) and no regrounding_token in l1_spine_registry.json. Requires a Governor re-grounding event (falsifiable claim + evidence + named downstream harm) before this trunk is valid."
            }
        }

        # Assert single-source: no OTHER file declares a competing precedence_rank list (FC-5)
        $searchRoots = @((Join-Path $PROJECT_ROOT ".claudecode"), $SPINE_TREE) | Where-Object { Test-Path $_ }
        $competingSources = Get-ChildItem -Path $searchRoots -Recurse -Include "*.json", "*.md" -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue |
            Where-Object { $_.FullName -ne $registryPath -and $_.FullName -notmatch '[\\/]node_modules[\\/]' } |
            ForEach-Object {
                $c = Get-Content $_.FullName -Raw -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
                if ($c -match '(?m)^\s*"?precedence_rank"?\s*[:=]') { $_.FullName }
            }
        foreach ($cs in $competingSources) {
            $relCs = $cs.Replace($PROJECT_ROOT, "").TrimStart("\", "/")
            $trunkSealFindings += "CHECK8: Competing precedence_rank source found at $relCs -- l1_spine_registry.json must be the SOLE source (FC-5)"
        }
    }

    Write-Output ""
    if ($trunkSealFindings.Count -gt 0) {
        Write-Output "L1 TRUNK SEAL (CHECK8):"
        foreach ($tsf in $trunkSealFindings) {
            Write-Output "  FAIL: $tsf"
        }
        $totalFindings += $trunkSealFindings.Count
        $findings += $trunkSealFindings
    } else {
        Write-Output "L1 TRUNK SEAL (CHECK8): PASS -- sealed set is single-source, no unsealed trunk found"
    }
    ```

- **`.claudecode/scripts/l1_trunk_creation_gate.ps1`** (NEW FILE — created and finalized this segment)
  - Full content:
    ```powershell
    # ============================================================
    # CSP L1 Trunk Creation Gate --Refusal-First (Governor S350)
    #
    # PURPOSE:
    #   Any attempt to create a NEW top-level spine (a 6th trunk) must pass
    #   through this gate. Default answer is NO. The only way to ALLOW is:
    #     (a) the proposed id is already one of the 5 sealed spines, or
    #     (b) l1_spine_registry.json carries a ratified regrounding_token
    #         for that id (Governor re-grounding event: falsifiable claim +
    #         evidence + named downstream harm --NOT a casual mint).
    #   Single source: .claudecode/scripts/l1_spine_registry.json (FC-5).
    #
    # USAGE:
    #   powershell -File l1_trunk_creation_gate.ps1 -ProposedSpineId "XYZ"
    #   Exit 0 = ALLOW. Exit 1 = REFUSE (the default).
    #
    # SCOPE NOTE: this gate only answers "may a new top-level trunk be
    # created?" It does not arbitrate a live cross-spine rule conflict at
    # runtime --that arbitration reads this registry once built later.
    #
    # SB-NNN | L1-SEAL-ENFORCEMENT-S350
    # ============================================================

    param(
        [Parameter(Mandatory = $true)][string]$ProposedSpineId
    )

    $ErrorActionPreference = 'Continue'
    [Console]::OutputEncoding = [System.Text.Encoding]::UTF8

    $SCRIPT_DIR = $PSScriptRoot
    if (-not $SCRIPT_DIR -or -not (Test-Path $SCRIPT_DIR -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue)) {
        $SCRIPT_DIR = "C:\Users\finky\Desktop\Claude Code\Core Sights Platform\.claudecode\scripts"
    }
    $registryPath = Join-Path $SCRIPT_DIR "l1_spine_registry.json"

    Write-Output "============================================================"
    Write-Output "L1 TRUNK CREATION GATE --refusal-first (default: NO)"
    Write-Output "============================================================"
    Write-Output "Proposed spine id: $ProposedSpineId"

    if (-not (Test-Path $registryPath)) {
        Write-Output "L1_TRUNK_GATE: REFUSE -- l1_spine_registry.json not found, cannot verify seal. Fail closed."
        exit 1
    }

    $registry = Get-Content $registryPath -Raw | ConvertFrom-Json
    $sealedIds = @($registry.spines | ForEach-Object { $_.id })

    if ($sealedIds -contains $ProposedSpineId) {
        Write-Output "L1_TRUNK_GATE: ALLOW -- '$ProposedSpineId' is one of the 5 already-sealed spines ($($sealedIds -join ', '))."
        exit 0
    }

    $regrounded = $registry.regrounding_tokens | Where-Object { $_.spine_id -eq $ProposedSpineId }
    if ($regrounded) {
        Write-Output "L1_TRUNK_GATE: ALLOW -- '$ProposedSpineId' carries a ratified regrounding_token (authorized_by: $($regrounded.authorized_by))."
        exit 0
    }

    Write-Output "L1_TRUNK_GATE: REFUSE -- creating new top-level spine '$ProposedSpineId' is BLOCKED by default."
    Write-Output "  The L1 seal (Governor S350) permits exactly 5 trunks: $($sealedIds -join ', ')."
    Write-Output "  A 6th trunk requires a Governor re-grounding event: a falsifiable claim the sealed set is"
    Write-Output "  wrong, PLUS evidence, PLUS a named downstream harm -- ratified and recorded as a"
    Write-Output "  regrounding_token in l1_spine_registry.json. No such token exists for '$ProposedSpineId'."
    exit 1
    ```

[USER] see if you can extract value from this 

Background removal is the digital process of isolating a foreground subject from its surroundings, either by making the background transparent or replacing it. It is essential for professional photography, e-commerce, and digital design. The most advanced solutions leverage deep learning and computer vision to automatically detect intricate details like hair, fur, and glass.The most advanced background removal technologies and solutions can be consolidated into the following primary groups:1. Dedicated Cloud APIs & SaaS PlatformsThese are highly optimized automated tools built to process large volumes of images quickly while handling extremely complex edges.Remove.bg: Long considered the industry standard for single-image tasks and hair-edge refinement. Access it at Remove.bg.Photoroom: A top choice for e-commerce and retail, utilizing sophisticated AI for edge precision, smart shadows, and generating marketplace-ready product backgrounds. Check it out at Photoroom.Aiarty Image Matting: Excels at extracting highly complex subjects (fur, hair) while simultaneously upscaling and de-blurring the output.2. Integrated Design & Workflow EcosystemsThese solutions are built for users who want to remove backgrounds as one step within a broader creative or publishing workflow.Canva: Ideal for social media creators and marketers, allowing users to remove backgrounds and immediately drop subjects into marketing templates. Available via Canva.Adobe Express (with Firefly AI): Integrates professional-grade, one-click removal tools that fit perfectly into the wider Adobe Creative Cloud ecosystem.3. Open-Source & Privacy-First Self-Hosted ToolsThese are developer-oriented or local tools used for projects that require maximum data privacy, zero usage costs, or custom integration.Rembg: The most popular open-source toolkit that allows developers to host their own background removal pipeline and call via APIs.Browser-Native Solutions (e.g., NoBG.space): Advanced client-side web tools that run entirely in your local browser, meaning absolutely no image data is uploaded to a remote server.4. End-to-End Generative AI & Video PlatformsAs content generation scales, many platforms now combine background removal with generative fill and motion graphic tracking.Invideo AI & Picsart: These platforms utilize state-of-the-art vision models and AI providers to isolate foreground subjects from both images and video, immediately dropping them into new scenes or applying dynamic marketing overlays.5. Pixel-Perfect Manual & Semi-Automated EditingFor high-end commercial retouching where algorithmic AI may still leave minor visual artifacts, manual and semi-automatic tools are required.Adobe Photoshop: Utilizes classic selection tools like the Magic Wand, but combines them with AI "Select and Mask" features to manually fine-tune details at the pixel level.

[ASSISTANT] Real, important question. Checked feasibility: we don't have Gemini API access configured yet (no key in `.env`, SDK not installed) — we've only ever tried rembg's built-in models (free, local, zero-cost). So no, we haven't tried the best available tool yet.

**On your two questions:**

1. **Are we using the best tool?** Probably not for the hard cases. rembg's models (u2net, isnet-general-use, birefnet) are pure *segmentation* models — they draw a boundary and cut. Gemini's image models (and similar generative image-editing models) work differently: they *regenerate* the image following an instruction, which is often much better at judgment calls like "this dark patch is a reflection, not the product" — exactly our stuck case. That matches what you saw.

2. **Should we train our own model?** No — not recommended, and I'd push back if asked to start that. Training/fine-tuning a segmentation model needs a labeled dataset (hand-drawn masks for many images), real compute, and real time — a heavy investment we shouldn't make before trying the much cheaper option (an existing generative model via API) that you already have anecdotal proof works in one shot.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
- Flask single-file page pattern: `@app.after_request` no-cache headers (`Cache-Control: no-store, no-cache, must-revalidate, max-age=0`) — mandatory on every page to prevent stale-cache-after-mutation bugs (bit this codebase twice this session).
- Data-driven hook dispatch pattern: `post_edit_check_dispatcher.ps1` + `hook_check_registry.json` — one hook registered once in `.claude/settings.json`, reads a JSON registry fresh every fire, so new checks never need a session restart (only the dispatcher itself needed one restart, once).
- `_load_history()`/`_load_data()` pattern: never silently return `{}`/empty on a corrupt read — raise loudly instead (learned from an incident this session, later flagged by the reliability expert-panel agent as a still-present real risk in dashboard_core_template.py's earlier version before a later fix... actually note: this was fixed in `dashboard_core_template.py`'s `_load_data()` via `raise RuntimeError(...)`, but the expert-panel review flagged this specific "swallow corruption" pattern as a real, unfixed risk in `pic_zf_review.py`'s `_load_history()` — two different files, only one fixed).
- Append-only version history: every classify/reclassify produces a NEW version in `pic_zf_history.json`, never overwrites.
- Reclassify-with-hint loop: `/respond` (in pic_zf_review.py) saves a correction comment on the CURRENT version first (so it's never lost), then calls `classify_single.py <path> <base> <hint>` as a subprocess, appending a genuinely new version with corrected fields — proven live to actually change output.
- `/api/reorganize` unified move+reorder pattern (dashboard_core_template.py): `mode='into'|'before'|'after'`, single endpoint handles cross-branch re-parenting AND same-level position changes, with a circular-move guard (`_is_descendant`).
- `/api/edit` direct field-edit pattern with server-side status-value validation against `STATUS_VALUES`.
- Excel-style inline cell editing: click a `.ct-cell` span → replaced with a real `<input>`/`<select>`, Enter/blur commits via `saveCellEdit()` → POST `/api/edit` → local `TREE` JS object updated → `renderTable()` re-render (no page reload for a cell edit).
- The "never hover-reveal controls" UX principle (newly discovered/fixed/memorialized this session as P-UX-C5): `display:none` + `:hover{display:flex}` on interactive controls causes real/perceived layout shift; fix = permanent visibility + hardened flex layout (`flex-shrink:0` on fixed-width siblings, `flex:1 1 auto; min-width:0` + `text-overflow:ellipsis` on the one field that should absorb space).
- Multi-agent expert panel pattern via the `Agent` tool: 5 parallel, independent `general-purpose` agents, each given the SAME standalone context brief file (no direct chat context), each with a distinct expert lens, each told to read real code themselves — synthesized afterward by the orchestrating session, not by the agents.
- `PRE_COMPACT_3_ITERATION_PROTOCOL_S350.md` — the permanent, hook-enforced (`pre_compact_3_iteration_check.ps1`) protocol requiring 3 passes before any compact: AI Defaults, Intents/Nuances/Insight, Processing Guarantee (tag every open item TYPE/STATUS/PROCESS-BY, never let it float across 2+ weekly-evolve-sessions with the same stale reason).
- `parked_items.json` / `Show-ParkedItemsDue` — the proven, already-live (no restart needed) daily-resurfacing mechanism for tracking deferred items with due dates; used in place of `CronCreate` (explicitly found unsuitable earlier — session-scoped, 7-day auto-expiry, not durable).

Backend routes (SECTION 3): `/api/upload/<id>`, `/api/download/<id>`, `/api/comment/<id>`, `/api/duplicate/<id>`, `/api/add`, `/api/edit/<id>` (validates status against `STATUS_VALUES`), `/api/reorganize` (unified move+reorder with `_is_descendant` circular-guard), `/api/delete/<id>` (soft-audit via `_append_audit`), `/api/export_all`. Data model helpers: `_load_data`/`_save_data`/`_find_node`/`_all_ids`/`_new_id`/`_reassign_ids`/`_now`/`_touch`/`_is_descendant`. `_touch()` stamps `modified_at` on every mutation; `created_at`/`modified_at` were backfilled onto all 34 original demo nodes.

Frontend (SECTION 4): three view modes (Tree/Table/Mindmap) via `switchView(viewName)` and `VIEW_NAMES = ['tree','table','mindmap']`. Table view (`renderTableHead`/`renderTableRows`/`renderTable`) is a genuine open spreadsheet grid — `.ct-cell` spans become real `<input>`/`<select>` on click via `startCellEdit()`, committed via `saveCellEdit()` (POST `/api/edit`, update local `TREE`, re-render, no page reload). Row-level heavy actions consolidated into one `.ct-menu-btn` ("⋮") + `.ct-menu-dropdown` per row (`CT_MENU_ACTIONS` array: upload/download/comment/duplicate/add/move/delete). Tree view drag-and-drop (`dragstart`/`dragover`/`drop` with 3-zone detection: top third=before, middle=into, bottom third=after) calling `/api/reorganize`. Move modal (`openMovePicker`) with quick up/down + searchable full re-parent picker (`flattenTree()`, `findSiblingIds()`). Edit modal (`openEditModal`/`submitEdit`) for label/note/tag/status/type. Mindmap: `mmExpandState`, node click-vs-drag disambiguation via `mmNodeDragId`/`mmNodeDragMoved` (movement >4px = real drag), `spacePressed` forces canvas pan even when starting on a node (`stopPropagation()` on node mousedown unless space held), wheel handler (`e.ctrlKey` = zoom, else pan) with `{passive:false}`.

```css
.row-actions button:hover { background:var(--border); color:var(--text); }
```
and:
```css
.node-row .toggle { ...; flex-shrink:0; }
.node-row .label { font-weight:600; flex-shrink:0; }
.node-row .note { color:var(--text-dim); font-size:12px; margin-left:6px; overflow:hidden; text-overflow:ellipsis;
  white-space:nowrap; min-width:0; flex:1 1 auto; }
.badge { ...; flex-shrink:0; }

.ct-cell:hover { background:var(--hover); }  /* removed the inset box-shadow ring */
```

4. Errors and fixes:
- **Pre-push gate blocked repeatedly** (`pre_push_hook.ps1` / `pre_push_gate.ps1`) — fix each time: add a small trailer commit whose message body contains the literal string `MID-SESSION-PUSH` (this is checked via `$allMessages -match 'MID-SESSION-PUSH'` across ALL unpushed commits, not just the latest) — this is NOT a session close (no BATCH_CLOSE tokens `[9.32]`-`[9.35]` needed). CRITICAL SIDE-DISCOVERY: `git add && git commit && git push` chained together in one Bash call gets the WHOLE chain blocked by the `PreToolUse:Bash` hook if it detects `push` anywhere in the command string — so `git commit` and `git push` must be run in SEPARATE Bash tool calls, not chained, or the commit itself silently never executes.
- **Corrupted Hebrew string literal**: `"שלח מ\u05砖וב"` (accidental mixed Chinese-Hebrew escape) caused a Python `SyntaxError: (unicode error) 'unicodeescape' codec can't decode`. Fixed by replacing with the correct literal Hebrew "משוב".
- **JS `\u{1F4CE}`/`\u{1F4AC}` unicode escapes inside a Python f-string** caused `SyntaxError: invalid decimal literal` because Python doesn't support the JS ES6 `\u{...}` codepoint syntax. Fixed by using Python's own `\U0001F4CE`/`\U0001F4AC` 8-digit escapes instead, which resolve to the literal emoji character before the JS ever reaches the browser.
- **Real UX validator finding**: `handleAction` had `fetch` calls with no `try/catch` (UXV-004). Fixed by wrapping the whole function body in try/catch plus a nested try/catch around the upload's async `onchange` callback.
- **My own `binary_fill_holes` bg-removal fix claim was incomplete and had to be retracted**: initially tested "safe" on 3 images that happened to trigger zero holes (false negative test coverage). When actually tested on `WA0067` (which DID have real base-transparency holes), the fix repaired the base but ALSO painted over legitimately-transparent crystal regions with the wrong background tone, creating a NEW visible defect. I explicitly told the user: "I need to walk back something I told you... I'm retracting 'tested safe' — it needs real refinement... before it touches production." Never shipped.
- **Table view v1 was the wrong design** — built 8 action-icon columns per Governor's literal words ("all the options... as columns"), but Governor corrected: "i meant an open table. like the regular simple ones with cells like excel." I explicitly said "no" to having a canned design pattern for this in the ux-expert skill (it's process-only), then built genuine inline cell editing.
- **Hover-triggered layout shift** (`.row-actions { display:none } .node-row:hover .row-actions { display:flex }`) — real, valid complaint ("everything is jumping on hover. worst ux"). Root-caused and fixed by making actions permanently visible + hardening the row's flex layout (`flex-shrink:0` on fixed elements, `flex:1 1 auto; min-width:0` + ellipsis on the note field) so permanence didn't introduce a new instability.
- **Stale server processes repeatedly caught live** (multiple times this session, on both port 5055 and while verifying dashboard_core_template on 5057) — the established discipline: always confirm a server's OS process `CreationDate` via `Get-CimInstance Win32_Process` before trusting its output, kill and restart when stale, never assume a `location.reload()` or browser refresh actually re-fetched fresh content without verifying via curl + fresh headers.

5. Problem Solving:
- Diagnosed the TAP-vs-TRI-E shape confusion down to a genuine taxonomy defect (empty `notes` field on TAP) rather than "the AI is guessing," using direct code reads of `shape_library.json` and `classifier_core.py`, and quantified the real (thin) extent of shape-value drift across actual production history.
- Ran a genuine 5-agent independent expert panel (not a single self-review) to avoid confirmation bias on "should the whole pipeline be rethought" — deliberately did not let the agents see each other's output, and flagged the one substantive disagreement (CV-first rebuild vs. fix-the-feedback-loop) as an open question for OPUS rather than resolving it unilaterally.
- Solved the "how do you prevent needing a restart for every new check" architecture question with a working, live-proven data-driven dispatcher pattern (registry JSON read fresh every fire) rather than just documenting the limitation.
- Solved the cross-branch re-parenting + precise reordering requirement with a single unified `/api/reorganize` endpoint (mode into/before/after) rather than building two separate mechanisms, and proved it works for the hardest case (cross-branch move + circular-move rejection) via direct API testing, not just UI clicking (which I cannot do myself).

## Educational template — proposed design (not yet built)

Point #15 of CSP's own checklist ("in-context tutorial — the logic of THIS step + best-practice advice, on-page not external") is exactly this ask, already named doctrine, not something to invent parallel to. Concrete design: extend the **same tooltip mechanism** I just built, but on the *template's own structural elements* (not just toolbar buttons) — hover the "Example Category" node and see "This is where you'd group related shapes/axes — see `cat-shape-triangular` in Shape Library for a real instance." Since every page inherits the whole engine verbatim on copy, these annotations propagate automatically — the template stops being a wireframe and starts teaching the pattern to whoever (human or AI) copies it next.

## The loop — real mechanisms already exist, scattered

Now, your two questions:

## How to restart (so the new hooks actually turn on)

Two options, try the lighter one first:

1. **Type `/hooks` and press enter** in this chat. That's a built-in command that reloads the hook configuration without closing anything. If it works, you're done — no need to restart.
2. **If `/hooks` doesn't do it**: close this chat/panel and open a new one (or reload the VS Code window: `Ctrl+Shift+P` → type "Reload Window" → enter). A brand-new session always picks up the current hook list fresh.

After either one, the way to confirm it worked (not just hope): ask me to check `.claudecode/scripts/.next_step_guard_debug.log` and `.ux_reaudit_guard_debug.log` after your next real message — if they have a fresh timestamp, it worked.

## Why this keeps happening, and how to stop needing it

Here's the actual mechanism, in plain terms: when Claude Code starts a session, it reads the list of hooks **once** and keeps that list in memory for the whole session. If I *edit the code inside* an already-listed hook, that's fine — it runs the file fresh every time, no restart needed. But if I *add a brand-new hook to the list* (which is what I did tonight, twice), the running session never looks at the list again to notice the new entry — hence the restart.

The good news: **this platform already solved this exact problem once, for a different kind of check** — `post_edit_check_dispatcher.ps1` is registered *once*, and instead of hardcoding what it checks, it reads a small JSON file (`hook_check_registry.json`) fresh every single time it runs. Adding check #10 there tomorrow means just adding one line to that JSON file — zero settings changes, zero restarts, ever.

Right now the "Stop" hooks (the ones that check my responses after I finish talking, including the 2 new ones from tonight) are NOT built that way — there are 5 separate ones individually listed. That's exactly why tonight needed 2 restarts and will need more every time a 6th, 7th, 8th check gets added.

**Recommendation:** consolidate those 5 into one `stop_hook_dispatcher.ps1` + a `stop_hook_registry.json`, copying the exact pattern that already works for `post_edit_check_dispatcher.ps1`. That's a real, moderate-sized fix — enhancing something already proven, not inventing anything new — and once it's done, no future Stop-hook addition will ever need a restart again. I'd like to build this next, unless you'd rather I hold and let the current 2 pending hooks get confirmed working first via the restart above.

Now, your harder question — let me actually think this through rather than reassure you.

**Do I over-check things?** Yes, honestly, this session had real instances:
- In the deep-dive, I ran 3 parallel agents to investigate hook/registry/validator state, then went back and **personally re-verified several things they'd already reported** (re-checking `context_guard.ps1` and `post_commit_validator.ps1` myself after Agent 1 already covered them). That's partly defensible — agents can be wrong, and I was about to *ship fixes* based on the findings — but I didn't need to re-verify everything they touched, just the parts I was about to act on.
- The stdin-JSON testing earlier took several failed attempts (bash escaping, then a scalar-vs-array PowerShell gotcha) before I got a working test harness. That's exploration cost, not file-reading waste, but it's real inefficiency.

**Am I hardwired to check "when was this file last changed" before deciding to read it?** No. And I want to push back on that specifically as the fix, not just answer "no."

An mtime-based skip-reading rule would be a bad idea on *this specific platform*, for a reason this exact session surfaced: files here get changed by things other than me reading and acting in a straight line — another AI tab (OPUS), or a hook running autonomously (we proved tonight that `post_commit_validator.ps1`, now that it actually works, auto-patches `SLIM_HANDOFF.md` on its own). "Unchanged since I last looked" is not the same as "still accurate" when other actors can touch the file without my session knowing. Caching on mtime would risk exactly the class of bug this whole session has been about — trusting a stale assumption because nothing *I* did to the file had changed.

What would actually help, practically:
1. **Scope agents tighter before fanning out** — so parallel agents don't redundantly read the same files, instead of me re-reading after the fact to double-check them.
2. **Keep using targeted reads** (grep first, then offset/limit reads) rather than whole-file reads — I mostly did this already, worth staying disciplined about.
3. Skip re-reading a file only when I can point to a *specific reason* it's still fresh — "I edited it two tool calls ago" (the harness already tracks this) — not a blanket timestamp rule.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
- **Template-family engine parity**: `dashboard_core_template.py`, `shape_library_page.py`, `csmse_classification_schema_page.py` must stay byte-identical in their generic engine (everything past SECTION 1 CONFIG); verified via `diff` after every change, propagated via a session-local, uncommitted `propagate_toolbar.py` script that preserves each page's own docstring/CONFIG/PAGE_VERSION while taking the rest from the template.
- **Real hover/focus tooltip component**: `.tt-wrap`/`.tt-popup` CSS + `_tb_control()` (Python, toolbar) / `ttWrap()` (JS, tree rows) helpers — position:absolute, non-reflowing, always-dark regardless of theme, with right-edge-clip protection for the rightmost element in a row/group. Explicitly distinguished from P-UX-C5 (which governs interactive controls disappearing, not supplementary info popups).
- **Hand-authored inline SVG icon set**: `ICONS` dict + `_SVG` lambda helper in `dashboard_core_template.py`, `currentColor` stroke-based line icons (not emoji) for a "platform tool" aesthetic.
- **Priority-ordered responsive breakpoint tiers**: Full/Compact/Condensed/Overflow, lowest-usage-frequency controls simplify first, documented in `RESPONSIVE_DESIGN_PROTOCOL_S350.md`.
- **Data-driven hook dispatcher pattern**: ONE hook registered in `.claude/settings.json`, reads a JSON registry fresh every fire — proven first by `post_edit_check_dispatcher.ps1` + `hook_check_registry.json` (PostToolUse), now replicated by `stop_hook_dispatcher.ps1` + `stop_hook_registry.json` (Stop) — the permanent fix for "every new check needs a session restart."
- **Always-fire debug trail pattern**: a hook must log SOMETHING on every invocation regardless of verdict, or "ran clean" and "never ran" are indistinguishable from outside — applied to `next_step_guard.ps1` and `ux_reaudit_guard.ps1`.
- **AST-based PowerShell verification**: `[System.Management.Automation.Language.Parser]::ParseFile($path, [ref]$tokens, [ref]$errors)` as the ONLY reliable way to detect silent parse failures — proven far more reliable than character-pattern heuristics (em-dash-in-string heuristic had both false positives and false negatives).
- **UTF-8 em-dash / PowerShell codepage desync bug class**: a valid UTF-8 em-dash byte sequence (`\xe2\x80\x94`) inside a double-quoted string in a `.ps1` file with no BOM gets misread under this Hebrew-Windows system's codepage, desyncing the parser and cascading into unrelated-looking downstream errors. Fix: replace with ASCII `--`.
- **Dead-flag anti-pattern**: `$script:blockPush = $true` set in 4 places in `pre_push_gate.ps1`, never read anywhere — the script always fell through to unconditional `exit 0`. General class: any "this blocks/fails X" flag needs to be traced to an actual consuming `if`.
- **PLAN_STANDARD gate architecture**: numbered gates in `PLAN_STANDARD_2026-03-28.md` prose vs. `# GATE N:` headers in `plan_standard_validator.ps1` — doc numbering has internal collisions; cross-reference by content/name, not number. Many "FAIL-CLOSED" claims in the doc have zero corresponding code ("vaporware").
- **PREVENTION_SYSTEM_S344.md's real structure**: numbered list (1-11) + `ST-N` (stability tail) + `G-N` (gaps-as-prevention-classes), 4-layer model (PLAN/IMPLEMENT/CHECK/SIMULATE) per failure class — NOT `FC-N` as a stale validator regex reference implied.
- **Chained bash command + PreToolUse(Bash) hook gotcha**: `git commit && git push` in one Bash call gets the WHOLE chain blocked if the hook detects `push` anywhere in the string, even if only the push portion would fail — commit and push must be separate tool calls.
- **Piped-command exit-code gotcha**: `command | tail -N; echo $?` captures `tail`'s exit code, not the piped command's — must redirect to a file and check `$?` immediately after the real command instead.
- **PowerShell `Get-Content` scalar-vs-array gotcha**: a single-line file returns a scalar string, not a 1-element array, causing `$lines[0]` to index into characters — synthetic test transcripts need ≥2 real lines to avoid this false signal.
- **80/10/10 session-pacing rule**: 80% primary build, 10% cleanup, 10% BATCH_CLOSE+handoff (5% absolute floor, no exceptions) — invoked explicitly when pushing back on doing more work at 7% remaining.
- **B_FILE_FIRST / GitHub link convention**: every actionable doc gets a `https://github.com/CommarkG/core-sights-platform/blob/main/<path>` link; confirmed remote is `https://github.com/CommarkG/core-sights-platform.git`.

4. Errors and fixes:
- **`_SVG` lambda viewBox bug**: `viewBox="0 0 {vb}"` with `vb` defaulting to `"0 0 16 16"` produced `"0 0 0 0 16 16"` — real browser console SEVERE errors on every icon. Found by actually running the mandatory post-implementation re-audit (checking `driver.get_log('browser')`), not skipped. Fixed by changing the default to `"16 16"`. No user feedback needed; self-caught via the gate I'd just built, which I explicitly framed as proof the gate has real value.
- **Favicon 404**: found in the same console-log check; fixed with a no-op `data:` favicon link.
- **Off-screen tooltip causing a permanent horizontal scrollbar**: found via `document.documentElement.scrollWidth` vs `window.innerWidth` measurement. First fix attempt (`overflow-x:hidden` on `body` only) did NOT work (re-measured, unchanged) — had to add it to `html` too. User feedback: none, self-caught and self-corrected via live re-measurement before declaring done.
- **PowerShell `Get-Content` scalar-vs-array gotcha**: single-line synthetic test transcripts caused `$lines[0]` to index into characters, producing misleading "line0: [{]"-type garbage. Fixed by using ≥2-line realistic synthetic transcripts for all subsequent hook pipe-tests.
- **UTF-8 em-dash / codepage parser desync**: root-caused via AST tokenizer dump (`[System.Management.Automation.PSParser]::Tokenize`) showing a garbled string token at line 274 of `post_commit_validator.ps1`. Initially misdiagnosed as possibly a missing here-string terminator or brace-count issue (Python regex-based brace counter, unreliable) before finding the real cause via direct token inspection. Fixed via byte-level replacement, verified via AST re-parse (0 errors) and live execution (real output).
- **`$script:blockPush` never read**: found while wiring in the new plan_standard_validator block, by grepping for all `blockPush` usages and finding the final `exit 0` was unconditional. Fixed by adding a check at the end. This directly affects push behavior for the whole team/session — verified extremely carefully before pushing (ran the gate live against real repo state, confirmed it correctly caught a real uncommitted-artifact case with the correct exit code, confirmed via a non-piped exit-code check after discovering the piped-`tail` exit-code gotcha).
- **Chained `git commit && git push` blocked by PreToolUse(Bash) hook**: happened once during the G6/HOOK_LIVENESS_AUDIT_AGENT commit — the whole chain got blocked because `push` was in the command string, meaning the commit never actually landed. Diagnosed via `git log -1` showing the old commit still HEAD; fixed by re-running `git commit` alone (no chained push), confirming it landed, then running `pre_push_gate.ps1` directly to see the REAL current finding (which turned out to be a legitimate uncommitted-artifact block, not the chained-command bug) before pushing separately.
- **Governor's efficiency/mtime-caching question**: not an error, but explicit user feedback requesting a self-critical, pushback-inclusive answer rather than reassurance. Answered honestly: acknowledged real inefficiencies (redundant agent+self re-verification) but explicitly pushed back against building an mtime-based read-skip mechanism, citing the concurrent-multi-tab-editing + autonomous-hook-mutation risk specific to this platform.

Graphify Behind the Wall — Safe-Isolation Install & Use Guide
A portable guide for AI coding assistants / collaborators. Goal: get the full value of Graphify while keeping it 100% isolated from your project's trusted and governance files. Read the reasoning — the "why" is the point, not just the commands.

Part 1 — The WALL principle (read this first)
Principle: external elements are USED, never TRUSTED. Use them to the maximum; isolate them to the maximum.

Why this matters, concretely:

Any external tool, model output, or third-party package can be wrong, stale, or evolve to reach further than you expected between the version you read about and the version you install. Its value can be real — but its authority over your project's truth must be zero.
An external tool must never: write into your always-loaded instruction/governance files (CLAUDE.md, AGENTS.md, .cursor/rules), insert itself into your trusted hooks, run a server/daemon, or make network calls you didn't ask for.
The wall = you use the tool's output from behind a boundary; the tool never crosses into your trusted state. You keep one source of truth; external output is an input to verification, never a fact.
The real example that motivates this guide: Graphify's default install writes its own rules into your CLAUDE.md (your governance root) and installs its own hooks — by default. That's a tool assuming it belongs in trusted territory. The wall says: use its graph, not its reach.
Two poles held together: MAX USAGE ⇄ MAX ISOLATION. Don't trade value for safety or safety for value — the method below gives both at once.

Part 2 — What Graphify is (and is not)
IS: a local structural knowledge graph of your codebase (tree-sitter parsing of code + docs), queryable from the CLI. Its value is token savings — it turns "grep/read the whole repo" into "query a compact graph." No account, no cloud.
IS NOT: an understanding of your project's meaning — decisions, statuses, rules, governance. It maps structure, not semantics. Treat every answer as a navigation hint, never authority. Verify against source before acting on anything load-bearing.
Part 3 — The MAX-ISOLATION install (do exactly this)
The safe path is Graphify's pure CLI mode — full graph + query value, zero reach into trusted files.

1. Install the CLI in an isolated environment:


uv tool install graphifyy
# alternatives if you lack uv:  pipx install graphifyy   (or)   pip install graphifyy
2. DO NOT run the integration installers — these are the wall breaches, skip them entirely:

✗ graphify install / graphify install --project → writes to CLAUDE.md / skill files in your trusted config
✗ graphify hook install → inserts Graphify's own hooks into your git/trusted path
✗ graphifyy[mcp] / python -m graphify.serve / any --mcp → runs an MCP server (external service surface). Keep zero external servers.
3. Generate the graph — local, no network, quarantined output:


graphify extract . --code-only
--code-only disables all network/LLM calls (tree-sitter parses source locally; nothing leaves your machine).
Output lands in graphify-out/. Add graphify-out/ to .gitignore and treat it as a quarantined artifact. Pass an explicit output path to keep it exactly where you want.
4. Query the graph manually — where the token-saving lives:


graphify query "<what you're looking for>"
graphify explain "<symbol or file>"
Use these instead of broad grep/find sweeps — same answer, a fraction of the tokens.

5. Keep it fresh under YOUR control: re-run graphify extract . --code-only after meaningful changes — as your own script/hook, not Graphify's installed hook.

Exact subcommands/flags can differ by version — run graphify --help and confirm the real syntax for your version. The principle (CLI-only, no install, code-only, quarantined output) is what matters.

Part 4 — Verify before you trust it (once, at your version)
Tools change between versions — verify actual behavior, not docs:

After graphify extract . --code-only, check what changed on disk: did it write only inside graphify-out/? Nothing in CLAUDE.md, AGENTS.md, .claude/, .cursor/, or git hooks?
Confirm no network connection was made.
If anything wrote outside the output dir, stop — that's a wall breach.
Pin the version you verified; re-verify on any upgrade (new versions can add new reach).
Part 5 — Why this exact setup (reasoning recap)
CLI-only + --code-only = maximum value (full graph + query, big token savings) with maximum isolation (no trusted-file writes, no network, no server).

Your own thin integration beats the tool's install (which reaches into your governance root). You decide what the tool touches — the whole point of the wall.
The graph is an external artifact behind the wall: consult it freely, trust it never. For anything that matters, verify against source.
This is how you get 100% of Graphify's usefulness and 0% of its intrusion.

Version note: Graphify was ~0.9.26 as of mid-2026; PyPI package graphifyy (double-y). Commands/flags evolve — confirm with graphify --help and re-run Part 4 after any update.
